	Since $D_L(\chi_Mu_0)\to0$ for each fixed $M$, we obtain
	\begin{align*}
		\limsup_{L\to\infty}D_L(u_0)
		\le 2\norm{u_0}_{\mathcal C_b(\mathbb{R})}e^{-\lambda M_0}
		+\frac{2\alpha T}{(M-M_0-1)^2}\norm{u_0}_{\mathcal C_b(\mathbb{R})}.
	\end{align*}
	Choosing first $M_0$ and then $M$ sufficiently large shows that $D_L(u_0)\to0$, proving~\eqref{ineq:initial semigroup convergence}.

	It remains to prove~\eqref{ineq:initial temporal interpolation gap}. By the definition of $\widehat A_L$, we see it holds for all $t \ge 0$ that
	\begin{align*}
		\norm{A_L(t)-\widehat A_L(t)}_{(\lambda)}
		&\le\norm{\pi_L^\text{int}(P^L_{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor+1}-P^L_{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor})u_L}_{(\lambda)}\\
		&{\overset{\eqref{ineq:continuous and discrete weighted norm}}{\le}}{e^{\lambda/L}\norm{P_{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor}^L(P_1^L-I)u_L}_{(\lambda),L}}\\
		&\le 2e^{\lambda/L}\exp\left\{\frac{\alpha T\lambda^2}{2}\cosh\lambda\right\}
		\norm{(P^L_1-I)u_L}_{(\lambda),L}.
	\end{align*}
	where we used Lemma~\ref{lem:weighted discrete semigroup stability} in the last inequality.

	Recall that $r_L=u_L-\pi_L^\text{disc}u_0$ and note that for any $f: \mathbb{Z}/L \to \mathbb{R}$ with $\norm{f}_{(\lambda), L}< \infty$,
	\begin{align*}
		e^{-\lambda|x_i|}\abs{f(x_{i\pm1})}
		\le e^{\lambda/L}\norm{f}_{(\lambda),L},\qquad \text{ for all  } x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L.
	\end{align*}
	Hence, it holds for all $L \in \mathbb{N}$ that
	\begin{align*}
		\norm{(P^L_1-I)r_L}_{(\lambda),L}
		&\le \alpha(1+e^{\lambda/L})\norm{r_L}_{(\lambda),L},\\
		\norm{(P^L_1-I)\pi_L^\text{disc}u_0}_{(\lambda),L}
		&\le \alpha\omega_{u_0,\lambda}(1/L).
	\end{align*}
	Since $u_L=r_L+\pi_L^\text{disc}u_0$, the preceding estimates imply that the following holds uniformly in $L \in \mathbb{N}$,
	\begin{align*}
		\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\norm{A_L(t)-\widehat A_L(t)}_{(\lambda)}
		\lesssim
		\norm{r_L}_{(\lambda),L}
		+\omega_{u_0,\lambda}(1/L),
	\end{align*}
	which proves~\eqref{ineq:initial temporal interpolation gap}.

	Finally, we show $\omega_{u_0,\lambda}(\delta)\to0$ as $\delta\downarrow0$ under the stated assumption $u_0\in\mathcal C_b(\mathbb{R})$. Indeed, given $\varepsilon>0$, choose $K>0$ such that $2\norm{u_0}_{\mathcal C_b(\mathbb{R})}e^{-\lambda K}<\varepsilon$. By uniform continuity of $u_0$ on $[-K-1,K+1]$, there exists $h_\varepsilon\in(0,1]$ such that $\abs{u_0(x)-u_0(y)}<\varepsilon$ whenever $x,y\in[-K-1,K+1]$ and $|x-y|\le h_\varepsilon$. For $\delta\le h_\varepsilon$, the part of~\eqref{eq:weighted_modulus_of_continuity} with $|x|\le K$ is therefore bounded by $\varepsilon$, while the part with $|x|>K$ is bounded by $2\norm{u_0}_{\mathcal C_b(\mathbb{R})}e^{-\lambda K}<\varepsilon$. Hence $\omega_{u_0,\lambda}(\delta)\le\varepsilon$, which completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:GRR}
	Let $K \subset \mathbb{R}^d$ be a compact set with Lipschitz boundary, let $p \in [1,\infty)$ and $s \in (0,1)$ so that $sp>d$. Then there exists $C>0$ depending only on $s,p$ and $K$ so that, for every continuous $f:K\to\mathbb{R}$,
	\begin{align*}
		\norm{f}_{\mathcal C_K^{\gamma}} \le C\Parentheses*{\norm{f}_{L^p_K}^p + \int_K \int_K \frac{\abs{f(x) - f(y)}^p}{\abs{x-y}^{d+sp}} \mathrm dx \mathrm dy}^\frac{1}{p}
	\end{align*}
	where $\gamma = \Parentheses*{sp - d} / p$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:holder norm bound uniform}
	For $m \in \mathbb{Z}$, denote $I_m = [m,m+1]$ and let $p>8$  and $\kappa_p>3$ be given as in~\eqref{ineq:p_moment bound}. Denote $Z_L(t,x):= \widetilde \xi^L_t(x) - \pi_L^\text{int}P^L_{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor}u_L(x)$, where $u_L:=\xi_0^L$, for each $L \in \mathbb{N}$, and $(t,x) \in \mathbb{R}_+\times \mathbb{R}$.  Set $\kappa:=\frac p4\wedge\frac{1+\kappa_p}{2}>2$. Then, for every $T>0$ and $0<\gamma<(\kappa-2)/p$,
	\begin{align*}
		\sup_{L} \sup_{m \in \mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E} \Bracket*{\sup_{t\in [0,T]}\norm{Z_L(t,\cdot)}_{\mathcal C_{I_m}^{\gamma}}^p} < \infty.
	\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Let  $\widetilde Z_L(t,x)$ be obtained through linearly interpolating in time of $Z_L$ between the points $t_i \in \mathbb{N}/L^2 \cup \{0\}$. Hence $\widetilde Z_L$ is a continuous random field on $\mathbb{R}_+\times \mathbb{R}$, and it is enough to prove the inequality for $\widetilde Z_L$.

	Fix an arbitrary $T>0$ and $0<\gamma<(\kappa-2)/p$,  set $\varsigma:=\gamma+2/p$, so that $2/p<\varsigma<\kappa/p$. Since $\frac p4\wedge(1+\kappa_p)\ge\kappa$, Propositions~\ref{prop:spatial_increment_moment_bound} and~\ref{prop:temporal_increment_moment_bound}, together with the triangle inequality, imply that
	\begin{align*}
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{\abs{Z_L(t_k,x_i)-Z_L(t_j,x_\ell)}^p}
		\lesssim
		\abs{t_k-t_j}^{\kappa}+\abs{x_i-x_\ell}^{\kappa}
	\end{align*}
	uniformly for $t_j,t_k\in L^{-2}\mathbb{Z}_+\cap[0,T+1]$, $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $x_i,x_\ell\in I_m\cap(\mathbb{Z}/L)$. Clearly, by the definition of linear interpolation, this implies that uniformly in $m$ and all sufficiently large $L$, it holds uniformly in $t,s \in [0,T]$, $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ and  $x,y \in I_m$ that
	\begin{align}\label{ineq:Z interpolated space time increment}
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{\abs{\widetilde Z_L(t,x)-\widetilde Z_L(s,y)}^p}
		\lesssim\Parentheses*{\abs{t-s}+\abs{x-y}}^{\kappa}.
	\end{align}
	Moreover, since $\widetilde Z_L(0,x)=0$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$, we see from~\eqref{ineq:Z interpolated space time increment} that
	\begin{align}\label{ineq:Z interpolated lp bound}
		\sup_L\sup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{
		\norm{\widetilde Z_L}_{L^p([0,T]\times I_m)}^p}
		<\infty.
	\end{align}
	Then by Lemma~\ref{lem:GRR},  it holds uniformly in $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ and sufficiently large $L\ge 1$ that
	\begin{align*}
		\mathbb{E} \Bracket*{\norm{\widetilde Z_L}_{\mathcal C_{[0,T]\times I_m}^{\gamma}}^p}&{\lesssim} {\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{\norm{\widetilde Z_L}_{L^p([0,T]\times I_m)}^p}+ \mathbb{E} \Bracket*{\iint_{[0,T]^2}\iint_{I_m\times I_m} \frac{\abs{\widetilde Z_L(t,x) -\widetilde Z_L(s,y)}^p}{\Parentheses*{\abs{t-s}+\abs{x-y}}^{2+\varsigma p}} \mathrm dx\mathrm dy \mathrm dt \mathrm ds}}\\
		&{\overset{\eqref{ineq:Z interpolated lp bound}}{\lesssim}} {1+ \iint_{[0,T]^2}\iint_{I_m\times I_m} \frac{\mathbb{E} \Bracket*{\abs{\widetilde Z_L(t,x) -\widetilde Z_L(s,y)}^p}}{\Parentheses*{\abs{t-s}+\abs{x-y}}^{2+\varsigma p}} \mathrm dx\mathrm dy \mathrm dt \mathrm ds}\\
		&{\overset{\eqref{ineq:Z interpolated space time increment}}{\lesssim}} {1+ \iint_{[0,T]^2}\iint_{I_m^2} \Parentheses*{\abs{t-s}+\abs{x-y}}^{\kappa - \varsigma p - 2}\mathrm dx\mathrm dy \mathrm dt \mathrm ds}\\
		&{<} {\infty},
	\end{align*}
	which is the desired result.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:holder-lebesgue-interpolation}
	Let $K \subset \mathbb{R}$ be any compact interval, let $p \ge 1$ and $\gamma \in (0,1]$, then for any continuous $f:K \to \mathbb{R}$, the following inequality holds
	\begin{align*}
		\norm{f}_{\mathcal C_K^0} \lesssim \norm{f}_{L^p_K}^{1-\theta}\norm{f}^\theta_{\mathcal C^\gamma_K}+\norm{f}_{L^p_K},
	\end{align*}
	where $\theta = \frac{1}{\gamma p+1}$, and the implicit constant depends only on $K$, $p\ge 1$ and $\gamma \in (0,1]$.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- thm-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:tightness}
	Suppose $\widetilde\xi_0^L\to u_0$ in $\mathcal C_\mathbb{R}^\mathrm{tem}$ for some $u_0\in\mathcal C_b(\mathbb{R})$, and write $u_L:=\xi_0^L$. Let $Y_L$ be the temporal linear interpolation of $\widetilde\xi^L$ between the points in $\mathbb{Z}_+/L^2$. Then $\{Y_L\}_{L\in\mathbb{N}}$ is tight in $\mathcal C(\mathbb{R}_+;\mathcal C_\mathbb{R}^\mathrm{tem})$.

	Let $p >8$ and $\kappa_p >3$ be as in~\eqref{ineq:p_moment bound}, set $\kappa:=\frac{p}{4}\wedge\frac{1+\kappa_p}{2}>2$. Then for every $T,\lambda>0$ and $0<\varepsilon<2(\kappa-2)$, it holds uniformly in $L \in \mathbb{N}$ that
	\begin{align}\label{ineq:full temporal interpolation gap}
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{
		\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
		\norm{\widetilde\xi_t^L-Y_L(t)}_{(\lambda)}^p}
		\lesssim
		L^{-2(\kappa-2)+\varepsilon}
		+\norm{u_L-\pi_L^\text{disc}u_0}_{(\lambda),L}^p
		+\omega_{u_0,\lambda}(1/L)^p.
	\end{align}
	As a consequence, $\{\widetilde\xi^L\}_{L\in\mathbb{N}}$ is tight in $\mathcal D(\mathbb{R}_+;\mathcal C_\mathbb{R}^\mathrm{tem})$, and all its limit points have continuous paths.
<!-- end thm-3 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Let $T,\lambda >0$ be arbitrary but fixed, define for $L \in \mathbb{N}$ and $(t,x) \in \mathbb{R}_+\times \mathbb{R}$ that
	\begin{align*}
		A_L(t,x):=\pi_L^\text{int}P^L_{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor}u_L(x),\qquad
		Z_L(t,x):=\widetilde\xi_t^L-A_L(t,x),
	\end{align*}
	and denote their temporal linear interpolations by $\widehat A_L$ and $\widehat Z_L$, respectively. By linearity,
	\begin{align}\label{eq:Y decomposition}
		Y_L=\widehat A_L+\widehat Z_L,
		\qquad
		\widetilde\xi^L-Y_L
		=(A_L-\widehat A_L)+(Z_L-\widehat Z_L).
	\end{align}

	We first estimate the stochastic remainder. Let $t_k:=k/L^2$ for $k \in \mathbb{Z}_+$ and denote
	\begin{align*}
		F_{k,L}:=Z_L(t_{k+1})-Z_L(t_k).
	\end{align*}
	We see by Proposition~\ref{prop:spatial_increment_moment_bound} and Lemma~\ref{lem:GRR} with $d=1$ that, for every $0<\gamma<\frac{\kappa-1}{p}$, it holds uniformly in  $L \in \mathbb{N},\, m \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $k \in \mathbb{Z}_+$ with $t_k \le T+1$ that
	\begin{align}\label{ineq:increment spatial holder Z}
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{\norm{F_{k,L}}_{\mathcal C_{I_m}^{\gamma}}^p}
		\lesssim 1.
	\end{align}
	On the other hand, since $t_{k+1}-t_k=L^{-2}$, Proposition~\ref{prop:temporal_increment_moment_bound} implies the following holds uniformly in $L \in \mathbb{N},\, k\in \mathbb{Z}_+$ and $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ with $t_k \le T+1$,
	\begin{align}\label{ineq:increment lp Z}
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{\norm{F_{k,L}}_{L^p_{I_m}}^p}
		\lesssim L^{-2\kappa}.
	\end{align}

	Let $\theta_{\gamma}:=(\gamma p+1)^{-1}$, then Lemma~\ref{lem:holder-lebesgue-interpolation}, H\"older's inequality,~\eqref{ineq:increment spatial holder Z}, and~\eqref{ineq:increment lp Z} imply the following holds uniformly in $L \in \mathbb{N},\, k\in \mathbb{Z}_+$ and $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ with $t_k \le T+1$,
	\begin{align*}
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{\norm{F_{k,L}}_{\mathcal C^0_{I_m}}^p}
		\lesssim
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{
		\norm{F_{k,L}}_{L^p_{I_m}}^{p(1-\theta_{\gamma})}
		\norm{F_{k,L}}_{\mathcal C_{I_m}^{\gamma}}^{p\theta_{\gamma}}}
		+\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{\norm{F_{k,L}}_{L^p_{I_m}}^p}\lesssim
		L^{-2\kappa(1-\theta_{\gamma})}
		+L^{-2\kappa}.
	\end{align*}
	For $t\in[t_k,t_{k+1}]$, the definition of temporal interpolation gives that, for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$,
	\begin{align*}
		\abs{Z_L(t,x)-\widehat Z_L(t,x)}
		\le\abs{F_{k,L}(x)}.
	\end{align*}
	Since $e^{-\lambda|x|}\lesssim e^{-\lambda|m|}$ uniformly in $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $x\in I_m$, we see it holds uniformly in $L \in \mathbb{N}$ that
	\begin{align*}
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
		\norm{Z_L(t)-\widehat Z_L(t)}_{(\lambda)}^p}
		&\lesssim
		\sum_{k\le(T+1)L^2}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
		e^{-\lambda p|m|}
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{\norm{F_{k,L}}_{\mathcal C^0_{I_m}}^p}\\
		&\lesssim
		L^{2-2\kappa(1-\theta_{\gamma})}
		+L^{2-2\kappa}.
	\end{align*}
	Note that $\kappa(1-\theta_{\gamma}) =\frac{\kappa\gamma p}{\gamma p+1} \longrightarrow\kappa-1$ as $\gamma\uparrow(\kappa-1)/p$. Hence, for every $\varepsilon>0$, we may choose $\gamma<(\kappa-1)/p$ sufficiently close to $(\kappa-1)/p$ so that the following holds uniformly in $L \in \mathbb{N}$,
	\begin{align}\label{ineq:stochastic temporal interpolation gap}
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
		\norm{Z_L(t)-\widehat Z_L(t)}_{(\lambda)}^p}
		\lesssim
		L^{-2(\kappa-2)+\varepsilon}.
	\end{align}

	Since $\widetilde\xi_0^L=\pi_L^\text{int}u_L$ and spatial interpolation agrees with $u_L$ on $\mathbb{Z}/L$, we see
	\begin{align*}
		\norm{u_L-\pi_L^\text{disc}u_0}_{(\lambda),L}
		\le\norm{\widetilde\xi_0^L-u_0}_{(\lambda)}
		\longrightarrow0,\qquad \text{ as  }L \to \infty.
	\end{align*}
	Lemma~\ref{lem:initial convergence} therefore applies and gives
	\begin{align}\label{ineq:initial gap used in tightness}
		\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
		\norm{A_L(t)-\widehat A_L(t)}_{(\lambda)}
		\lesssim\norm{u_L-\pi_L^\text{disc}u_0}_{(\lambda),L}
		+\omega_{u_0,\lambda}(1/L),
	\end{align}
	uniformly in $L \in \mathbb{N}$. Therefore, by~\eqref{eq:Y decomposition},~\eqref{ineq:stochastic temporal interpolation gap},~\eqref{ineq:initial gap used in tightness},
	\begin{align*}
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
		\norm{\widetilde\xi_t^L-Y_L(t)}_{(\lambda)}^p}
		\lesssim
		L^{-2(\kappa-2)+\varepsilon}+\norm{u_L-\pi_L^\text{disc}u_0}_{(\lambda),L}^p
		+\omega_{u_0,\lambda}(1/L)^p,
	\end{align*}
	which proves~\eqref{ineq:full temporal interpolation gap}.

	Finally, Lemma~\ref{lem:initial convergence} also shows that $\widehat A_L\to P_\cdot u_0$ in $\mathcal C(\mathbb{R}_+;\mathcal C_\mathbb{R}^\mathrm{tem})$, uniformly on compact time intervals. Propositions~\ref{prop:spatial_increment_moment_bound} and~\ref{prop:temporal_increment_moment_bound}, together with \cite[Lemma 6.3]{shiga1994two}, imply that $\{\widehat Z_L\}_{L\in\mathbb{N}}$, hence  $\{Y_L\}_{L\in\mathbb{N}}$, is tight in $\mathcal C(\mathbb{R}_+;\mathcal C_\mathbb{R}^\mathrm{tem})$.

	By Lemma~\ref{lem:initial convergence}, the right hand side of~\eqref{ineq:full temporal interpolation gap} converges to zero for $0<\varepsilon<2(\kappa-2)$. Hence $\widetilde\xi^L-Y_L\to0$ in probability, uniformly on compact time intervals in $\mathcal C_\mathbb{R}^\mathrm{tem}$. The tightness of $\{\widetilde\xi^L\}_{L\in\mathbb{N}}$ in $\mathcal D(\mathbb{R}_+;\mathcal C_\mathbb{R}^\mathrm{tem})$ follows.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:equicontinuity}
	Suppose $\widetilde\xi_0^L\to u_0$ in $\mathcal C_\mathbb{R}^\mathrm{tem}$ for some $u_0\in\mathcal C_b(\mathbb{R})$, and set $u_L:=\xi_0^L$. Let $p>8$ be as in~\eqref{ineq:p_moment bound}. Then, for every $K\ge1$ and $T>0$,
	\begin{align*}
		\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{
		\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{\abs{x_i}\le K\atop x_i\in\mathbb{Z}/L}
		\abs{\xi_t^L(x_i)-\xi_t^L(x_{i+1})}^p}=0.
	\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Define for $t \ge 0$ and $L \in \mathbb{N}$ that
	\begin{align*}
		A_L(t):=\pi_L^\text{int}P^L_{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor}u_L,\qquad
		Z_L(t):=\widetilde\xi_t^L-A_L(t).
	\end{align*}
	Note that $\widetilde\xi_0^L=\pi_L^\text{int}u_L(x_i) = u_L(x_i)$ for all $x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L$. Hence for every $\lambda >0$,
	\begin{align*}
		\norm{u_L-\pi_L^\text{disc}u_0}_{(\lambda),L}
		\le\norm{\widetilde\xi_0^L-u_0}_{(\lambda)}
		\longrightarrow0,\qquad \text{ as }L \to \infty.
	\end{align*}
	So Lemma~\ref{lem:initial convergence} applies. Note that for $t\in[0,T]$ and $x_i\in\mathbb{Z}/L$,
	\begin{align*}
		\abs{\xi_t^L(x_i)-\xi_t^L(x_{i+1})}
		&\le
		\abs{A_L(t,x_i)-A_L(t,x_{i+1})}
		+\abs{Z_L(t,x_i)-Z_L(t,x_{i+1})}.
	\end{align*}

	\noindent We first consider the initial-semigroup term.  We see for any $\lambda >0$,
	\begin{align*}
		\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
		\sup_{\abs{x_i}\le K\atop x_i\in\mathbb{Z}/L}
		\abs{A_L(t,x_i)-A_L(t,x_{i+1})}&\le
		2e^{\lambda(K+1)}
		\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\norm{A_L(t)-P_tu_0}_{(\lambda)}\\
		&\qquad+
		\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
		\sup_{\substack{|x-y|\le L^{-1}\\|x|,|y|\le K+1}}
		\abs{P_tu_0(x)-P_tu_0(y)}.
	\end{align*}
	The first term on the right hand side converges to zero by Lemma~\ref{lem:initial convergence}. Since $(t,x)\mapsto P_tu_0(x)$ is continuous, it is uniformly continuous on $[0,T]\times[-K-1,K+1]$, so the second term also converges to zero. Consequently,
	\begin{align*}
		\lim_{L\to \infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
		\sup_{\abs{x_i}\le K\atop x_i\in\mathbb{Z}/L}
		\abs{A_L(t,x_i)-A_L(t,x_{i+1})}
		=0.
	\end{align*}

	It remains to control the stochastic remainder.  Let $\mathcal I_K:=\{m\in\mathbb{Z}:I_m\cap[-K,K+1]\neq\varnothing\}$. Every pair $x_i,x_{i+1}$ with $|x_i|\le K$ is contained in some $I_m$ with $m\in\mathcal I_K$. Therefore, by Lemma~\ref{lem:holder norm bound uniform}, there exists some $\gamma >0$ so that the following holds uniformly in $L \in \mathbb{N}$,
	\begin{align*}
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{
		\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
		\sup_{\abs{x_i}\le K\atop x_i\in\mathbb{Z}/L}
		\abs{Z_L(t,x_i)-Z_L(t,x_{i+1})}^p}\le L^{-\gamma p}
		\sum_{m\in\mathcal I_K}
		\mathbb{E}\Bracket*{
		\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
		\norm{Z_L(t,\cdot)}_{\mathcal C^\gamma_{I_m}}^p}
		\lesssim L^{-\gamma p}.
	\end{align*}
	Combining the preceding estimates gives the desired result.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- prop-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:limiting martingale}
	For any $\varphi \in \mathcal C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R})$ the process $(M_t(\varphi))_{t \ge 0}$ defined as in~\eqref{eq:mtg phi limit} is a continuous, mean zero, square integrable martingale with respect to the natural filtration of $\xi$. In particular, let $p >8$ be as in~\eqref{ineq:p_moment bound}, then  for any $T>0$
	\begin{align}\label{eq:lp convergence of mtg term}
		\lim_{L \to \infty}  \norm{\sup_{t \in [0,T]}\abs{\widetilde M^L_t(\varphi) - M_t(\varphi)}}_{L^p_\Omega} = 0.
	\end{align}
<!-- end prop-8 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
We shall fix an arbitrary $T >0$ in this proof. We note that continuity of $M_t(\varphi)$ in $t\ge 0$ is trivial since all terms on the right hand side of~\eqref{eq:mtg phi limit} are continuous in time.

	Let $\varphi \in \mathcal C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R})$ and $K \ge 1$ so that $\mathrm{supp}\Parentheses*{\varphi} \subset [-K,K]$. For any $L \ge 1$, recall $\rho_L$ from~\eqref{eq:interpolation convolution identity}. Consider by Fubini's theorem and summation by parts that
	\begin{align*}
		\left \langle\pi^\mathrm{int}_L\Parentheses*{  \Delta_L \xi_s^L}, \varphi\right \rangle &= \int_\mathbb{R} \frac{1}{L}\sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \Delta_L\xi^L_s(x_i) \rho_L(x - x_i) \varphi(x) \mathrm dx\\
		&= \frac{1}{L}\sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L}\Delta_L\xi^L_s(x_i)  \int_\mathbb{R} \rho_L(x- x_i ) \varphi(x) \mathrm dx  \\
		&=\frac{1}{L} \sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \Delta_L \xi_s^L(x_i) \int_\mathbb{R} \rho_L(x) \varphi(x+x_i) \mathrm dx\\
		&=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \xi_s^L(x_i) \int_\mathbb{R} \rho_L(x) \Parentheses*{\varphi(x+x_{i+1}) -2\varphi(x+x_i) +\varphi(x+x_{i - 1})}\mathrm dx \\
		&=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \xi_s^L(x_i) \int_\mathbb{R} \rho_L(x - x_i) \Parentheses*{\varphi\Parentheses*{x+\frac{1}{L}} - 2\varphi(x) + \varphi\Parentheses*{x - \frac{1}{L}} }\mathrm dx \\
		&= \frac{1}{L} \sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \xi_s^L(x_i) \int_\mathbb{R} \rho_L(x-x_i) \Delta_L\varphi(x) \mathrm dx \\
		&=\int_\mathbb{R} \Parentheses*{\frac{1}{L} \sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \xi_s^L(x_i) \rho_L(x - x_i)  }  \Delta_L \varphi(x) \mathrm dx   \\
		&=\left \langle \widetilde \xi_s^L, \Delta_L \varphi\right \rangle.
	\end{align*}
	Therefore, for any $t>0$
	\begin{equation}\label{ineq: martingale difference}
		\begin{aligned}
			\abs{M_t(\varphi) - \widetilde M_t^L(\varphi)} &\le  \abs{  \left\langle  \xi_t-  \xi_0, \varphi \right \rangle -\left\langle \widetilde \xi^L_t- \widetilde \xi_0^L, \varphi \right \rangle}+\int_0^{\frac{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor}{L^2}} \frac{\alpha}{2} \abs{\left \langle   \xi_s-\widetilde \xi^L_s, \Delta \varphi\right \rangle} \mathrm ds \\
			&\qquad + \int_0^{\frac{\lfloor tL^2 \rfloor}{L^2}} \abs{ \left\langle b(\xi_s) - b(\widetilde \xi_s^L), \varphi\right\rangle} \mathrm ds + E_1^L(t)+E_2^L(t)+E_3^L(t),
		\end{aligned}
	\end{equation}
	where
	\begin{align*}
		E_1^L(t) &=\int_0^{\frac{\lfloor tL^2 \rfloor}{L^2}}\frac{\alpha}{2} \abs{\left \langle \widetilde \xi^L_s, \frac{\Delta_L}{L^{-2}}\varphi - \Delta \varphi \right \rangle }\mathrm ds\\
		E_2^L (t)&=\int_0^{\frac{\lfloor tL^2 \rfloor}{L^2}} \abs{\left \langle\widetilde B_L\Parentheses*{\xi_s^L}- b\Parentheses*{\widetilde \xi^L_s}, \varphi \right \rangle} \mathrm ds\\
		E_3^L(t)&=\int_{\frac{\lfloor tL^2 \rfloor}{L^2}}^t \frac{\alpha}{2}\abs{\langle \xi_s, \Delta \varphi \rangle} + \abs{\langle b(\xi_s), \varphi \rangle}\mathrm ds.
	\end{align*}

	We see by boundedness of $\xi$, $\xi^L$ and the fact that $\varphi \in \mathcal C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R})$ that
	\begin{align*}
		\limsup_{L \to \infty}\sup_{t \in [0,T]} E_3^L(t) + E_1^L(t) = 0,\qquad \text{ almost surely. }
	\end{align*}
	Let us denote for $f: [0,1] \to \mathbb{R}$ that
	\begin{align}\label{eq:modulus of continuity on unit interval}
		\omega_f^0(\delta):=\sup\left\{\abs{f(u)-f(v)}:u,v\in[0,1],\ \abs{u-v}\le \delta\right\},\qquad \text{ for }\delta \ge 0.
	\end{align}
	Since $b$ is continuous on $[0,1]$,  we see $\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\omega_b^0(\delta)=0$.

	Let $x \in \mathbb{R}$, then there exists $x_i = i/L \in \mathbb{Z}/L$ so that  $x=\tau x_i+(1-\tau)x_{i+1}$ for some $\tau\in[0,1]$. The definitions of $\widetilde B_L$ and linear interpolation give
	\begin{align*}
		\abs{\widetilde B_L\Parentheses*{\xi_s^L}(x)-b\Parentheses*{\widetilde\xi_s^L(x)}}\le \sup_{y\in[0,1]}\abs{b_L(y)-b(y)}
		+\omega_b^0\left(\max_{-1\le j\le1}
		\abs{\xi_s^L(x_{i+j+1})-\xi_s^L(x_{i+j})}\right).
	\end{align*}
	Consequently,
	\begin{align*}
		\sup_{\abs{x}\le K}
		\abs{\widetilde B_L\Parentheses*{\xi_s^L}(x)-b\Parentheses*{\widetilde\xi_s^L(x)}}\le \sup_{y\in[0,1]}\abs{b_L(y)-b(y)}
		+\omega_b^0\left(\sup_{\substack{\abs{x_i}\le K+1\\x_i\in\mathbb{Z}/L}}
		\abs{\xi_s^L(x_{i+1})-\xi_s^L(x_i)}\right).
	\end{align*}
	Lemma~\ref{lem:equicontinuity}, the boundedness of $\omega_b^0$, and dominated convergence therefore imply
	\begin{align}
		\limsup_{L\to\infty}\norm{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}E_2^L(t)}_{L^p_\Omega}
		&\lesssim \norm{\varphi}_{L^1_\mathbb{R}}\limsup_{L\to\infty}
		\left\{\sup_{y\in[0,1]}\abs{b_L(y)-b(y)}\right.\nonumber\\
		&\left.\qquad+\norm{\omega_b^0\left(\sup_{\substack{s\in[0,T],\ \abs{x_i}\le K+1\\x_i\in\mathbb{Z}/L}}
		\abs{\xi_s^L(x_{i+1})-\xi_s^L(x_i)}\right)}_{L^p_\Omega}\right\}=0.
	\end{align}
	By the almost-sure convergence obtained from Theorem~\ref{thm:tightness}, for every $K\ge1$, it holds that
	\begin{align*}
		\limsup_{L \to \infty}\sup_{t \in [0,T]} \sup_{\abs{x} \le K} \abs{\widetilde\xi_t^L(x) - \xi_t(x)} = 0,\qquad \text{almost surely.}
	\end{align*}
	Hence, inserting the previous estimates into~\eqref{ineq: martingale difference}, we see by the uniform boundedness of $\xi^L,\xi$ and the dominated convergence theorem, it holds that
	\begin{align*}
		\lim_{L \to \infty}\norm{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
		\abs{\widetilde M_t^L(\varphi)-M_t\Parentheses*{\varphi}}}_{L^p_\Omega}
		\lesssim&
		\lim_{L\to \infty}\bigg(\norm{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{\abs{x}\le K}
		\abs{\widetilde\xi_t^L(x)-\xi_t(x)}}_{L^p_\Omega}\\
		&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\norm{\omega_b^0\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{\abs{x}\le K}
		\abs{\widetilde\xi_t^L(x)-\xi_t(x)}\right)}_{L^p_\Omega}\bigg)\\
		=&0,
	\end{align*}
	which proves~\eqref{eq:lp convergence of mtg term}.

	Moreover, $\widetilde M^L(\varphi)$ is adapted to the natural filtration of $\widetilde\xi^L$ and is a martingale with respect to that filtration, while $M(\varphi)$ is adapted to the natural filtration of $\xi$. Since $p>1$,~\eqref{eq:lp convergence of mtg term} and Theorem~\ref{thm:tightness} yield the joint convergence in law
	\begin{align*}
		\Parentheses*{\widetilde\xi^L,\widetilde M^L(\varphi)}
		\Longrightarrow \Parentheses*{\xi,M(\varphi)}.
	\end{align*}
	In addition, by the uniform boundedness of $\widetilde \xi^L$ and $\xi$ and of $b_L$ for large $L$ (by~\eqref{eq:coefficient convergence}),   $\{\widetilde M_t^L(\varphi)\}_L$ is uniformly integrable for every $t\ge0$. Therefore, by \cite{ethier2009markov}*{Chapter 7, Problem 7, p.~362} with $E=\mathcal C^\mathrm{tem}_\mathbb{R}$,  $M(\varphi)$ is a martingale with respect to the natural filtration of $\xi$. Its square integrability follows again from~\eqref{eq:lp convergence of mtg term}, and it has mean zero because $M_0(\varphi)=0$.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:quadratic variation prelimit}
	Let $L \ge 1$, $\varphi,\psi \in \mathcal C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R})$ and $(\widetilde M_t^L (\varphi))_t$ be as in~\eqref{eq:mtg varphi prelimit}.  Then
	\begin{align*}
		[\widetilde M^L(\varphi),\widetilde M^L(\psi)]_t
		=\int_0^{\frac{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor}{L^2}}\frac{1}{L} \sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L}
		\widehat{\varphi}_L(x_i)\widehat{\psi}_L(x_i)
		\Gamma_L\Parentheses*{\xi_{s}^L}(x_i) \mathrm ds,
		\qquad 0\le t<\infty\quad\text{a.s.}
	\end{align*}
	In addition, almost surely in $0\le t < \infty$,
	\begin{align*}
		\abs{[\widetilde M^L(\varphi),\widetilde M^L(\psi)]_t
		-\int_0^{\frac{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor}{L^2}}
		\left\langle\varphi\psi,\widetilde\Gamma_L\Parentheses*{\xi_s^L}\right\rangle\mathrm ds}
		\le t\omega_\psi\Parentheses*{ 2L^{-1}}
		\norm{\varphi}_{L^1_\mathbb{R}}\sup_{y\in[0,1]}\sigma_L(y),
		\qquad
	\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Recall $\eta^L$ in~\eqref{eq:martingale decomposition one step} for $L \ge 1$, then by~\eqref{eq: weak formulation prelimit} and Fubini's theorem, it holds for all $t \ge 0$ and $L \ge 1$ that
	\begin{align*}
		\widetilde M^L_t(\varphi)&= \sum_{k = 1}^{{\lfloor tL^2 \rfloor}} \int_\mathbb{R} \frac{1}{L}\sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L}  \eta^L_k(x_i) \rho_L(x_i-x) \varphi(x) \mathrm dx\\
		&=\sum_{k = 1}^{{\lfloor tL^2 \rfloor}}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L}  \eta^L_k(x_i) \int_\mathbb{R}  \rho_L(x_i-x) \varphi(x) \mathrm dx\\
		&=:\sum_{k = 1}^{{\lfloor tL^2 \rfloor}}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L}  \eta^L_k(x_i) \widehat{\varphi}_L(x_i).
	\end{align*}
	Hence, by conditional independence, we see
	\begin{align*}
		[\widetilde M^L(\varphi),\widetilde M^L(\psi)]_t &= \sum_{k = 1}^{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor}\frac{1}{L^2}\mathbb E \Bracket*{\Parentheses*{ \sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \eta^L_k(x_i)\widehat{\varphi}_L(x_i)   }\Parentheses*{ \sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \eta^L_k(x_i)\widehat{\psi}_L(x_i)   } \mid \mathcal F_{k-1}}\\
		&=\sum_{k = 1}^{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor}\frac{1}{L^2} \sum_{x_i,x_j \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \widehat{\varphi}_L(x_i)\widehat{\psi}_L(x_j) \mathbb{E} \Bracket*{\eta^L_k(x_i) \eta^L_k(x_j)\mid \mathcal F_{k-1}}\\
		&=\sum_{k = 1}^{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor  } \frac{1}{L^2} \sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \widehat \varphi_L(x_i)\widehat \psi_L(x_i) \mathbb{E}\Bracket*{\eta^L_k(x_i)^2\mid \mathcal F_{k-1}}\\
		&\overset{\eqref{eq:mtg_increment l2} }{=} \frac{1}{L^2}\sum_{k = 0}^{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor-1} \frac{1}{L}\sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \widehat{\varphi}_L (x_i)\widehat{\psi}_L (x_i) \Gamma_L\Parentheses*{\xi_{k/L^2}^L}(x_i)\\
		&= \int_0^{\frac{\lfloor tL^2\rfloor}{L^2}} \frac{1}{L} \sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \widehat{\varphi}_L(x_i)\widehat{\psi}_L (x_i) \Gamma_L\Parentheses*{\xi_{s}^L}(x_i) \mathrm ds,
	\end{align*}
	which proves the first part of the Lemma.

	For the second part, since $\int_\mathbb{R} \rho_L(x) \mathrm dx = 1$ and $\mathrm{supp}(\rho_L) \subset[-1/L,1/L]$ for all $L \ge 1$, we see for $x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L$,
	\begin{align*}
		\abs{\widehat \varphi_L(x_i)\widehat{\psi}_L (x_i) - \widehat{\varphi\cdot\psi}_L(x_i)}&= \abs{\iint_{\mathbb{R}^2} \varphi(x)\psi(y) \rho_L(x-x_i)\rho_L(y - x_i) \mathrm dy \mathrm dx - \int_{\mathbb{R}} \varphi(x)\psi(x) \rho_L(x-x_i) \mathrm dx}\\
		&=\abs{\iint_{\mathbb{R}^2} \varphi(x) \Parentheses*{\psi(y) - \psi(x)}\rho_{L}(x-x_i) \rho_L(y - x_i) \mathrm dy \mathrm dx}\\
		&\le \iint_{\mathbb{R}^2} \abs{\varphi(x)} \abs{\psi(y) - \psi(x)}\rho_{L}(x-x_i) \rho_L(y - x_i) \mathrm dy \mathrm dx\\
		&= \iint_{[x_i - 1/L,x_i+1/L]^2}\abs{\varphi(x)} \abs{\psi(y) - \psi(x)}\rho_{L}(x-x_i) \rho_L(y - x_i) \mathrm dy \mathrm dx\\
		&\le \omega_\psi\Parentheses*{2L^{-1}} \int_\mathbb{R} \abs{\varphi(x)} \rho_L(x- x_i) \mathrm dx\\
		&= \omega_\psi\Parentheses*{2L^{-1}} \widehat{\abs{\varphi} }_L(x_i).
	\end{align*}
	Therefore,
	\begin{align*}
		\abs{\frac{1}{L}\sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \widehat\varphi_L(x_i) \widehat{\psi}_L(x_i) \Gamma_L\Parentheses*{\xi^L_s}(x_i) - \left \langle \varphi\psi, \widetilde \Gamma_L\Parentheses*{\xi^L_s} \right \rangle}&\le \frac{1}{L} \sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L } \abs{\widehat \varphi_L(x_i)\widehat{\psi}_L(x_i)  - \widehat{\varphi\cdot \psi}_L(x_i)}\Gamma_L\Parentheses*{\xi^L_s}(x_i)\\
		&\le \omega_\psi\Parentheses*{2L^{-1}} \frac{1}{L} \sum_{x_i \in \mathbb{Z}/L} \widehat{\abs{\varphi}}_L(x_i) \Gamma_L\Parentheses*{\xi^L_s}(x_i)\\
		&\le \omega_\psi(2L^{-1}) \norm{\varphi}_{L^1_\mathbb{R}}\sup_{y\in[0,1]}\sigma_L(y),
	\end{align*}
	Indeed,~\eqref{eq:R assumption} gives
	\begin{align*}
		\Gamma_L(\xi_s^L)(x_i)
		=L\operatorname{Var}_{x}\Parentheses*{\mathcal R^L(x)-x}
		\bigg|_{x=\xi_s^L(x_i)+\frac{\alpha}{2}\Delta_L\xi_s^L(x_i)},
	\end{align*}
	and hence $0\le\Gamma_L(\xi_s^L)(x_i)\le\sup_{y\in[0,1]}\sigma_L(y)$. Integrating with respect to $s$ proves the second assertion.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- prop-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:characterization of limiting martingale}
	Let $\varphi,\,\psi\in\mathcal C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R})$. Then
	\begin{align*}
		\left[M(\varphi) ,M(\psi) \right]_t = \int_0^t \langle \sigma(\xi_s), \varphi\psi\rangle\mathrm ds,\qquad t \ge 0.
	\end{align*}
<!-- end prop-9 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
For $t\ge0$ and $\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R})$, set
	\begin{align*}
		A_t(\varphi,\psi):=\int_0^t\langle\sigma(\xi_s),\varphi\psi\rangle\,\mathrm ds.
	\end{align*}
	Since $A(\varphi,\psi)$ is continuous, adapted, and of finite variation, it is predictable. By uniqueness of predictable covariation, it therefore suffices to show that $M(\varphi)M(\psi)-A(\varphi,\psi)$ is a martingale. For each $L$, the corresponding compensated product
	\begin{align*}
		N_t^L:=\widetilde M_t^L(\varphi)\widetilde M_t^L(\psi)
		-\Bracket*{\widetilde M^L(\varphi),\widetilde M^L(\psi)}_t,\qquad t \ge 0
	\end{align*}
	is a martingale. Write $N_t:=M_t(\varphi)M_t(\psi)-A_t(\varphi,\psi)$. We will prove that, for every $T>0$, $N^L$ converges to $N$ uniformly on $[0,T]$ in $L^2(\Omega)$; equivalently,
	\begin{align*}
		\lim_{L \to \infty}\norm{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\abs{\Parentheses*{\widetilde M^L_t(\varphi) \widetilde M^L_t(\psi) - \Bracket*{\widetilde M^L(\varphi),\widetilde M^L(\psi)}_t} - \Parentheses*{M_t(\varphi)M_t(\psi) -\int_0^t \langle \sigma(\xi_s), \varphi\psi\rangle \mathrm ds}}}_{L^2_\Omega} =0.
	\end{align*}
	By~\eqref{eq:lp convergence of mtg term} and H\"older's inequality, the product terms converge uniformly on $[0,T]$ in $L^2(\Omega)$. Moreover, Lemma~\ref{lem:quadratic variation prelimit} gives
	\begin{align*}
		\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\abs{\Bracket*{\widetilde M^L(\varphi),\widetilde M^L(\psi)}_t-A_t(\varphi,\psi)}
		&\le T\omega_\psi(2L^{-1})\norm{\varphi}_{L^1_\mathbb{R}}
		\sup_{y\in[0,1]}\sigma_L(y)\\
		&\qquad+\int_0^{\frac{\lceil TL^2\rceil}{L^2}}
		\left\langle\abs{\widetilde\Gamma_L(\xi_s^L)-\sigma(\xi_s)},
		\abs{\varphi\psi}\right\rangle\mathrm ds\\
		&\qquad+L^{-2}\norm{\varphi\psi}_{L^1_\mathbb{R}}\sup_{y\in[0,1]}\sigma(y).
	\end{align*}
	By~\eqref{eq:coefficient convergence}, $\sup_{y\in[0,1]}\sigma_L(y)$ is uniformly bounded for all sufficiently large $L$. Hence the first and third terms on the right hand side converge to zero. It therefore remains to prove
	\begin{align}\label{eq: char of limiting martingale done}
		\lim_{L \to \infty}  \norm{\int_0^{\frac{\lceil TL^2\rceil}{L^2}} \left \langle \abs{\widetilde \Gamma_L(\xi_s^L) - \sigma(\xi_s)}, \abs{\varphi\psi}\right \rangle \mathrm ds}_{L^2_\Omega} = 0.
	\end{align}
	Recall $\omega_f^0$ from~\eqref{eq:modulus of continuity on unit interval}. Since $\sigma$ is continuous on $[0,1]$, $\omega_\sigma^0(r)\to0$ as $r\downarrow0$. Suppose $\operatorname{supp}(\varphi\psi)\subset[-K,K]$ for some $K\ge1$. If $x\in[x_i,x_{i+1}]$ for some $x_i = i/L$, then the definition of $\widetilde\Gamma_L$ and spatial linear interpolation give
	\begin{align*}
		\abs{\widetilde\Gamma_L\Parentheses*{\xi_s^L}(x)-\sigma\Parentheses*{\widetilde\xi_s^L(x)}}\le \sup_{y\in[0,1]}\abs{\sigma_L(y)-\sigma(y)}
		&+L^{-3}\sup_{y\in[0,1]}\abs{b_L(y)}^2\\
		&+\omega_\sigma^0\left(\max_{-1\le j\le1}
		\abs{\xi_s^L(x_{i+j+1})-\xi_s^L(x_{i+j})}\right).
	\end{align*}
	Therefore, for all sufficiently large $L$,
	\begin{align*}
		\sup_{\substack{s\in[0,T+1]\\\abs{x}\le K}}
		\abs{\widetilde\Gamma_L\Parentheses*{\xi_s^L}(x)-\sigma\Parentheses*{\xi_s(x)}}&\le \sup_{y\in[0,1]}\abs{\sigma_L(y)-\sigma(y)}+L^{-3}\sup_{y\in[0,1]}\abs{b_L(y)}^2\\
		&\qquad\quad+\omega_\sigma^0\left(
		\sup_{\substack{s\in[0,T+1],\ \abs{x_i}\le K+1\\x_i\in\mathbb{Z}/L}}
		\abs{\xi_s^L(x_{i+1})-\xi_s^L(x_i)}\right)\\
		&\qquad\quad+\omega_\sigma^0\left(
		\sup_{\substack{s\in[0,T+1]\\\abs{x}\le K}}
		\abs{\widetilde\xi_s^L(x)-\xi_s(x)}\right).
	\end{align*}
	By Lemma~\ref{lem:equicontinuity}, the third term converges to zero in $L^p(\Omega)$. The almost-sure local uniform convergence $\widetilde\xi^L\to\xi$ implies that the fourth term converges to zero almost surely. The first two terms in the preceding bound converge to zero by~\eqref{eq:coefficient convergence}. Therefore, the boundedness and continuity of $\omega_\sigma^0$ imply that the right hand side converges to zero in $L^2(\Omega)$ as $L \to \infty$. Since $\lceil TL^2\rceil/L^2\le T+1$, we see~\eqref{eq: char of limiting martingale done} holds.

	Consequently, $N^L\to N$ uniformly on $[0,T]$ in $L^2(\Omega)$. Together with the almost-sure convergence $\widetilde\xi^L\to\xi$, this yields
	\begin{align*}
		(\widetilde\xi^L,N^L)\Longrightarrow(\xi,N),
	\end{align*}
	and the $L^2$ convergence implies that $\{N_t^L\}_L$ is uniformly integrable for every $t\ge0$. Moreover, $N^L$ is a martingale with respect to the natural filtration of $\widetilde\xi^L$, while $N$ is adapted to the natural filtration of $\xi$. Hence \cite{ethier2009markov}*{Chapter 7, Problem 7, p.~362} implies that $N$ is a martingale with respect to the natural filtration of $\xi$. The uniqueness of predictable covariation therefore gives $[M(\varphi),M(\psi)]=A(\varphi,\psi)$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
We recall the standard argument for completeness. Fix $n\in\mathbb{N}$, test functions $\varphi_1,\dots,\varphi_n\in\mathcal C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R})$, and $F\in\mathcal C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n)$. Set
	\begin{align*}
		Z_t^i:=\langle\xi_t,\varphi_i\rangle,
		\qquad Z_t:=\Parentheses*{Z_t^1,\dots,Z_t^n}.
	\end{align*}
	Recall $M$ from~\eqref{eq:mtg phi limit}, Proposition~\ref{prop:limiting martingale} gives
	\begin{align*}
		Z_t^i=Z_0^i+\int_0^t
		\frac{\alpha}{2}\langle\xi_s,\Delta\varphi_i\rangle
		+\langle b(\xi_s),\varphi_i\rangle\mathrm ds+M_t(\varphi_i).
	\end{align*}
	Note that since $\varphi_i$'s have compact support, $(Z_t^i)_{t \ge 0}$ is a continuous square integrable semi-martingale for each $i = 1,\dots, n$. By Proposition~\ref{prop:characterization of limiting martingale}, we see
	\begin{align*}
		[M(\varphi_i),M(\varphi_j)]_t
		=\int_0^t\langle\sigma(\xi_s),\varphi_i\varphi_j\rangle\,\mathrm ds.
	\end{align*}
	Therefore, It\^o's formula shows that
	\begin{align*}
		F(Z_t)-F(Z_0)
		&-\int_0^t\sum_{i=1}^n\partial_iF(Z_s)
		\left\{
		\frac{\alpha}{2}\langle\xi_s,\Delta\varphi_i\rangle
		+\langle b(\xi_s),\varphi_i\rangle
		\right\}\mathrm ds\\
		&-\frac12\int_0^t\sum_{i,j=1}^n\partial_{ij}F(Z_s)
		\langle\sigma(\xi_s),\varphi_i\varphi_j\rangle\,\mathrm ds
	\end{align*}
	is a mean zero martingale in $t\ge 0$. Hence the law of $\xi$ solves the  martingale problem associated with~\eqref{eq: spde target}. By \cite{iwata1987infinite}*{Theorem 3.3}, on an extension of the underlying probability space there exists a space--time white noise $W$ such that $\xi$ is a weak solution of~\eqref{eq: spde target}. Weak uniqueness for~\eqref{eq: spde target} therefore identifies the law of every subsequential limit with the law of its unique weak solution. Together with tightness, this proves the convergence of the full sequence.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Suppose $n = 2k$ for some $k \ge 1$. Then, by Cauchy--Schwarz,
	\begin{align*}
		p_{2k}(x,y) &= \sum_z p_k(x,z)p_k(z,y)\\
		& \le \Parentheses*{\sum_z p_k(x,z)^2}^\frac{1}{2}\Parentheses*{\sum_z p_k(y,z)^2}^\frac{1}{2}\\
		&=\sqrt{p_{2k}(x,x)}\sqrt{p_{2k}(y,y)}\\
		&=p_{2k}(0,0)\\
		&\lesssim k^{-1/2},
	\end{align*}
	where we used Proposition~\ref{prop:lawler-242} in the last inequality.

	Suppose now $n = 2k+1$ for some $k \ge 1$. Then
	\begin{align*}
		p_{2k+1}(x,y)  = \sum_z p_{2k}(x,z)p_1(z,y) \le \sup_z p_{2k}(x,z) =p_{2k}(0,0) \lesssim k^{-1/2} \lesssim\Parentheses*{2k+1}^{-1/2}.
	\end{align*}
	The case $n=1$ is immediate.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Consider $n\in \mathbb{N}$ that
	\begin{align*}
		\sum_z\Parentheses*{p_n(x,z) - p_n(y,z)}^2  &\le \sum_z \abs{p_n(x,z)-p_n(y,z)}\Parentheses*{p_n(x,z)+p_n(y,z)}\\
		& \le 2\sup_{u,v} p_n(u,v) \sum_z \abs{p_n(x,z)-p_n(y,z)}\\
		&\lesssim n^{-1/2}\frac{\abs{x-y}}{n^{1/2}},
	\end{align*}
	where we used Proposition~\ref{prop:lawler-242}  and Corollary~\ref{cor:heat kernel on diagonal bound} in the last line.

	On the other hand, by Proposition~\ref{prop:lawler-242} again, we see
	\begin{align*}
		\sum_z\Parentheses*{p_n(x,z) - p_n(y,z)}^2& = \sum_z\Parentheses*{p_n(x,z)^2-2p_n(x,z)p_n(y,z)+p_n(y,z)^2}\\
		&\le p_{2n}(x,x)+p_{2n}(y,y)\\
		&=2p_{2n}(0,0)\lesssim n^{-1/2}.
	\end{align*}
	Finally, the semigroup property implies that, for $n,h\ge1$ and $x \in \mathbb{Z}$,
	\begin{align*}
		\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\abs{p_{n+h}(x,z)-p_n(x,z)}
		&\le\sum_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}p_h(x,w)
		\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\abs{p_n(w,z)-p_n(x,z)}\\
		&{\overset{\eqref{ineq:discrete-heat-kernel-gradient-abs-difference}}{\lesssim}} {n^{-1/2}\sum_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}p_h(x,w)\abs{w-x}}\\
		&\le n^{-1/2}
		\left(\sum_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}p_h(x,w)\abs{w-x}^2\right)^{1/2}\\
		&=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha h}{n}},
	\end{align*}
	where we used Jensen's inequality in the third line and the fact that $\mathbb{E}_x[(S_h-S_0)^2]=\alpha h$ in the last line. Together with the trivial bound by $2$, this proves~\eqref{ineq:heat kernel temporal l1 difference}. This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
By translation invariance we may assume $x=0$. By the semigroup property, we see it holds for all $n,k \in \mathbb{N}$ that
	\begin{align*}
		\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigl(p_{n+k}(0,y)-p_n(0,y)\bigr)^2
		&= \sum_y p_{n+k}(0,y)^2-2\sum_y p_{n+k}(0,y)p_n(0,y)+\sum_y p_n(0,y)^2\\
		&= p_{2n+2k}(0,0)-2p_{2n+k}(0,0)+p_{2n}(0,0).
	\end{align*}
	By Lemma~\ref{lem:local clt} with $x=0$, for all $m,h\ge 1$,
	\begin{align*}
		\abs{p_{m+h}(0,0)-p_m(0,0)}
		&\le \abs{p_{m+h}(0,0)-(2\pi\alpha(m+h))^{-1/2}}\\
		&\qquad +\abs{(2\pi\alpha(m+h))^{-1/2}-(2\pi\alpha m)^{-1/2}}\\
		&\qquad +\abs{(2\pi\alpha m)^{-1/2}-p_m(0,0)}\\
		&\lesssim (m+h)^{-3/2}+hm^{-3/2}+m^{-3/2}
		\lesssim hm^{-3/2}.
	\end{align*}
	Consequently,
	\begin{align*}
		p_{2n+2k}(0,0)-2p_{2n+k}(0,0)+p_{2n}(0,0)&\le \abs{p_{2n+2k}(0,0)-p_{2n+k}(0,0)}
		+\abs{p_{2n+k}(0,0)-p_{2n}(0,0)}\\
		&\lesssim k(2n+k)^{-3/2}+k(2n)^{-3/2}\\
		&\lesssim kn^{-3/2},
	\end{align*}
	as desired.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Let $(S_k)_{k\ge0}$ be the lazy symmetric random walk introduced in Section~\ref{sec:discrete heat kernel}. Its increments $X_1,X_2,\ldots$ are independent and satisfy
	\[
		\mathbb{P}(X_j=1)=\mathbb{P}(X_j=-1)=\frac{\alpha}{2},
		\qquad
		\mathbb{P}(X_j=0)=1-\alpha.
	\]
	By translation invariance, for $x_i=i/L$,
	\[
		P_k^Lf(x_i)
		=\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(x_i+\frac{S_k}{L}\right)\right].
	\]
	Consequently,
	\begin{align*}
		e^{-\lambda|x_i|}\abs{P_k^Lf(x_i)}
		&\le
		\norm{f}_{(\lambda),L}
		\mathbb{E}\left[
		e^{\lambda\left(
		|x_i+S_k/L|-|x_i|
		\right)}
		\right] \\
		&\le
		\norm{f}_{(\lambda),L}
		\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda|S_k|/L}\right].
	\end{align*}

	For every $\theta\in\mathbb{R}$,
	\[
		\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta X_1}]
		=\frac{\alpha}{2}e^\theta+(1-\alpha)
		+\frac{\alpha}{2}e^{-\theta}
		=1-\alpha+\alpha\cosh\theta.
	\]
	Since $S_k=X_1+\cdots+X_k$, independence gives
	\[
		\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta S_k}]
		=\bigl(1-\alpha+\alpha\cosh\theta\bigr)^k.
	\]
	Using $e^{|x|}\le e^x+e^{-x}$ and the symmetry of $S_k$, we obtain
	\begin{align*}
		\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda|S_k|/L}\right]
		&\le
		\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda S_k/L}\right]
		+\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-\lambda S_k/L}\right] \\
		&=2\bigl(1-\alpha+\alpha\cosh(\lambda/L)\bigr)^k \\
		&\le
		2\exp\left\{
		\alpha k\bigl(\cosh(\lambda/L)-1\bigr)
		\right\},
	\end{align*}
	where the last inequality follows from $1+x\le e^x$.

	For $z\ge0$,
	\[
		\cosh z-1
		=\int_0^z(z-s)\cosh s\,\mathrm ds
		\le \frac{z^2}{2}\cosh z.
	\]
	Therefore, since $L\ge1$ and $k\le TL^2$,
	\begin{align*}
		\alpha k\bigl(\cosh(\lambda/L)-1\bigr)
		&\le
		\frac{\alpha k\lambda^2}{2L^2}\cosh(\lambda/L) \\
		&\le
		\frac{\alpha T\lambda^2}{2}\cosh\lambda.
	\end{align*}
	It follows that
	\[
		\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda|S_k|/L}\right]
		\le
		2\exp\left\{
		\frac{\alpha T\lambda^2}{2}\cosh\lambda
		\right\}.
	\]
	Taking the supremum over $x_i\in\mathbb{Z}/L$ completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Write $\abs{f}_{\mathcal C_K^\gamma}:=\sup_{x\neq y\in K}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{|x-y|^\gamma}$. Let $x\in K$ and $r\in(0,1]$ and $I:=K\cap[x-r,x+r]$ and note $|I|\gtrsim r$. Then
	\begin{align*}
		|f(x)|
		&\le \frac{1}{|I|}\int_I |f(y)|\,\mathrm dy + \frac{1}{|I|}\int_I |f(x)-f(y)|\,\mathrm dy\\
		&\le |I|^{-1/p}\norm{f}_{L^p_K} + r^\gamma\abs{f}_{\mathcal C_K^\gamma}\\
		&\lesssim r^{-1/p}\norm{f}_{L^p_K} + r^\gamma\abs{f}_{\mathcal C_K^\gamma},
	\end{align*}
	where the upper bound is independent of $x$. Taking supremum over $x\in K$ gives
	\begin{align*}
		\norm{f}_{\mathcal C_K} \lesssim r^{-1/p}\norm{f}_{L^p_K}+ r^\gamma\abs{f}_{\mathcal C_K^\gamma}.
	\end{align*}
	Choosing $r:=\min\left\{1,\left(\norm{f}_{L^p_K}/\abs{f}_{\mathcal C_K^\gamma}\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma+1/p}}\right\}$ yields $\norm{f}_{\mathcal C_K}\lesssim \norm{f}_{L^p_K}^{1-\theta}\abs{f}_{\mathcal C_K^\gamma}^\theta+\norm{f}_{L^p_K}$ with $\theta=\frac{1}{\gamma p+1}$. Finally, $\abs{f}_{\mathcal C_K^\gamma}\le \norm{f}_{\mathcal C_K^\gamma}$, so the stated bound follows.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
